spectree/spec.py:133
  /testbed/spectree/spec.py:133: Pyd<#sep#>You are given a CSV file named `sales.csv` containing sales data for a retail store. Each row in the file represents a sale and includes the following fields: `date`, `product_id`, `quantity`, and `price`. Your task is to write a function `calculate_total_sales(file_path)` that reads the CSV file, computes the total sales (quantity * price) for each product, and returns a dictionary where the keys are `product_id` and the values are the total sales for that product.

**Input Format:**
- The CSV file `sales.csv` will have the following columns: `date`, `product_id`, `quantity`, and `price`.
- `date` is a string in the format `YYYY-MM-DD`.
- `product_id` is a string representing the unique identifier of the product.
- `quantity` is an integer representing the number of units sold.
- `price` is a float representing the price per unit.

**Output Format:**
- A dictionary where each key is a `product_id` and each value is the total sales for that product.

**Sample Input:**
```
date,product_id,quantity,price
2023-01-01,A001,10,19.99
2023-01-02,A002,5,29.99
2023-01-03,A001,7,19.99
2023-01-04,A003,3,49.99
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{'A001': 359.87, 'A002': 149.95, 'A003': 149.97}
```

```python
import csv

def calculate_total_sales(file_path):
    """
    Reads a CSV file containing sales data and calculates the total sales for each product.

    Parameters:
    file_path (str): The path to the CSV file.

    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary with product_id as keys and total sales as values.
    """
    total_sales = {}
    
    with open(file_path, mode='r') as file:
        csv_reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        
        for row in csv_reader:
            product_id = row['product_id']
            quantity = int(row['quantity'])
            price = float(row['price'])
            sale_amount = quantity * price
            
            if product_id in total_sales:
                total_sales[product_id] += sale_amount
            else:
                total_sales[product_id] = sale_amount
    
    return total_sales

“Yes” because any other path between 1 and n must go via the added edges or the path edges. But added edges only have higher weight. 

Specifically, all edges added between non-consecutive nodes have weights that are very large (like 1e9), so any path through them would have total weight greater than P. 

Thus, the path we constructed is the shortest (path sum P), so that holds. 

Thus, this approach would work. 

The steps:

1. Find the smallest prime P such that P ≥n. 

2. Set all the path edges except the last edge's initial edges (the first (n-2 edges) to 1, and the last edge's weight is (P - (n-2)), so that total is P. 

Thus, the last edge's weight is P - (n-2). Here n-2 comes from (n-1) edges minus last edge. 

For example:

If n=4: P is 5. P - (4-2) =5-2=3. 

Thus, the edges between 1-2:1, 2-3:1, 3-4:3 → sum 5. 

Thus, okay. 

Now, for m edges:

- The path has (n-1) edges, so need to generate m - (n-1) edges. 

Each of these can be any edge between two non-consecutive nodes (or any pair not directly connected by path edges) with a weight 1e9. 

Thus, a systematic way to generate them:

For example, connect nodes 1 to node3, node1 to node4 (if applicable), and so on. 

But to avoid loops or multiedges, we must ensure only single edges between unconnected path nodes.

Here's a way to choose edges:

Start from node1, connect to all nodes except node2 (as already connected via the path). 

Then node2, connect to node4 (if it's not consecutive), etc. 

Alternatively, for each pair (u, v) where u ≠ v, and |u-v| >1 (so not adjacent on path), and that the edge is new, we can add them one by one until we have the needed edges.

<#sep#>You are given a list of Python code snippets, where each snippet is a string in a list named `code_snippets`. Your task is to write a function `format_code(snippets)` that takes this list as input, formats each code snippet according to PEP 8 guidelines, and returns a list of formatted code snippets. Use the `autopep8` library for formatting.

**Input:**
- A list of strings, where each string is a Python code snippet.

**Output:**
- A list of strings, where each string is the corresponding formatted Python code snippet.

**Sample Input:**
```python
code_snippets = [
    "def myfunction(x,y):\n return x+y",
    "for i in range(10):print(i)"
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[
    "def myfunction(x, y):\n    return x + y",
    "for i in range(10):\n    print(i)"
]
```

```python
import autopep8

def format_code(snippets):
    """
    Formats each code snippet in the input list according to PEP 8 guidelines.

    Parameters:
    snippets (list of str): A list of Python code snippets.

    Returns:
    list of str: A list of formatted Python code snippets.
    """
    return [autopep8.fix_code(snippet) for snippet in snippets]

# Example usage
code_snippets = [
    "def myfunction(x,y):\n return x+y",
    "for i in range(10):print(i)"
]

formatted_code_snippets = format_code(code_snippets)
print(formatted_code_snippets)

def is_prime(x):
    if x < 2:
        return False
    if x ==2:
        return True
    if x%2 ==0:
        return False
    for i in range(3, int(math.sqrt(x)) +1, 2):
        if x%i ==0:
            return False
    return True

    70	        :strict:    collect all the routes decorated by this instance
    71	        """
    72	        if self.config.MODE == 'greedy':
    73	            return False
    74	        elif self.config.MODE == 'strict':
    75	            if getattr(func, '_decorator', None) == self:
    76	                return False
    77	            return True
    78	        else:
    79	            decorator = getattr(func, '_decorator', None)
    80	            if decorator and decorator != self:
    81	                return True
    82	            return False
    83	
    84	    def validate(self,
    85	                 query=None, json=None, headers=None, cookies=None, resp=None, tags=(),
    86	                 before=None, after=None):
    87	        """
    88	        - validate query, json, headers in request
    89	        - validate response body and status code
    90	        - add tags to this API route
    91	
    92	        :param query: `pydantic.BaseModel`, query in uri like `?name=value`
    93	        :param json: `pydantic.BaseModel`, JSON format request body
    94	        :param headers: `pydantic.BaseModel`, if you have specific headers
    95	        :param cookies: `pydantic.BaseModel`, if you have cookies for this route
    96	        :param resp: `spectree.Response`
    97	        :param tags: a tuple of tags string
    98	        :param before: :meth:`spectree.utils.default_before_handler` for specific endpoint
    99	        :param after: :meth:`spectree.utils.default_after_handler` for specific endpoint
   100	        """
   101	
   102	        def decorate_validation(func):
   103	            # for sync framework
   104	            @wraps(func)
   105	            def sync_validate(*args, **kwargs):
   106	                return self.backend.validate(
   107	                    func,
   108	                    query, json, headers, cookies, resp,
   109	                    before or self.before, after or self.after,
   110	                    *args, **kwargs)
   111	
   112	            # for async framework
   113	            @wraps(func)
   114	            async def async_validate(*args, **kwargs):
   115	                return await self.backend.validate(
   116	                    func,
   117	                    query, json, headers, cookies, resp,
   118	                    before or self.before, after or self.after,
   119	                    *args, **kwargs)
   120	
   121	            validation = async_validate if self.backend_name == 'starlette' else sync_validate
   122	
   123	            # register
   124	            for name, model in zip(('query', 'json', 'headers', 'cookies'),
   125	                                   (query, json, headers, cookies)):
   126	                if model is not None:
   127	                    assert (issubclass(model, BaseModel))
   128	                    self.models[model.__name__] = model.schema()
   129	                    setattr(validation, name, model.__name__)
   130	
   131	            if resp:
   132	                for model in resp.models:
   133	                    self.models[model.__name__] = model.schema()
   134	                validation.resp = resp
   135	
   136	            if tags:
   137	                validation.tags = tags
   138	
   139	            # register decorator
   140	            validation._decorator = self
   141	            return validation
   142	
   143	        return decorate_validation
   144	
   145	    def _generate_spec(self):
   146	        """
   147	        generate OpenAPI spec according to routes and decorators
   148	        """
   149	        routes, tags = {}, {}
   150	        for route in self.backend.find_routes():
   151	            path, parameters = self.backend.parse_path(route)
   152	            routes[path] = routes.get(path, {})
   153	            for method, func in self.backend.parse_func(route):
   154	                if self.backend.bypass(func, method) or self.bypass(func):
   155	                    continue
   156	
   157	                name = parse_name(func)
   158	                summary, desc = parse_comments(func)
   159	                func_tags = getattr(func, 'tags', ())
   160	                for tag in func_tags:
   161	                    if tag not in tags:
   162	                        tags[tag] = {'name': tag}
   163	
   164	                routes[path][method.lower()] = {
   165	                    'summary': summary or f'{name} <{method}>',
   166	                    'operationID': f'{name}__{method.lower()}',
   167	                    'description': desc or '',
   168	                    'tags': getattr(func, 'tags', []),
   169	                    'parameters': parse_params(func, parameters[:], self.models),
   170	                    'responses': parse_resp(func),
   171	                }
   172	
   173	                request_body = parse_request(func)
   174	                if request_body:
   175	                    routes[path][method.lower()]['requestBody'] = request_body
   176	
   177	        spec = {
   178	            'openapi': self.config.OPENAPI_VERSION,
   179	            'info': {
   180	                'title': self.config.TITLE,
   181	                'version': self.config.VERSION,
   182	            },
   183	            'tags': list(tags.values()),
   184	            'paths': {**routes},
   185	            'components': {
   186	                'schemas': {**self.models}
   187	            },
   188	            'definitions': self._get_model_definitions()
   189	        }
   190	        return spec
   191	
   192	    def _get_model_definitions(self):
   193	        """
   194	        handle nested models
   195	        """
   196	        definitions = {}
   197	        for schema in self.models.values():
   198	            if 'definitions' in schema:
   199	                for key, value in schema['definitions'].items():
   200	                    definitions[key] = value
   201	                del schema['definitions']
   202	
   203	        return definitions
   204

```python
def sort_and_filter(numbers):
    """
    Processes a list of integers by removing duplicates and sorting the list in ascending order.

    Parameters:
    numbers (list of int): The list of integers to be processed.

    Returns:
    list of int: A sorted list of integers with duplicates removed.
    """
    return sorted(set(numbers))

import sys
import math

def is_prime(x):
    if x < 2:
        return False
    if x ==2:
        return True
    if x%2==0:
        return False
    for i in range(3, int(math.sqrt(x))+1, 2):
        if x%i ==0:
            return False
    return True

def next_prime(x):
    if x ==2:
        return 2
    candidate = x if x%2 !=0 else x+1
    while True:
        if is_prime(candidate):
            return candidate
        candidate +=2

def main():
    n, m = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    # Find the smallest prime P >= n-1
    current = n -1
    P = next_prime(current)
    # Now construct the edges for the path:
    path = []
    for i in range(n-1):
        if i < n-2:
            w = 1
        else:
            w = P - (n-2) # since first n-2 edges sum is n-2 (each 1) so nth term is (P - (n-2))
        path.append( (i+1, i+2, w) )
    # Now make sure the last edge's weight is correct:
    # Need to have the path edges' sum equal to P.
    # compute the sum of path edges:
    total = sum( w for u, v, w in path )
    #assert total == P, (total, P)
    # Now generate the remaining edges:
    edges_needed = m - (n-1)  
    # get edges that are not in path:
    non_path_edges = []
    # all possible edges not in the path. 
    for u in range(1, n+1):
        for v in range(u+2, n+1):
            non_path_edges.append( (u, v, 10**9) )
    # also check in reverse? since undirected, but the order doesn't matter
    # now pick edges_needed edges. 
    selected = []
    for edge in non_path_edges:
        if len(selected) < edges_needed:
            selected.append( edge )
        else:
            break

    all_edges = path + selected

    assert len(all_edges) == m, (len(all_edges), m)

    #check for multiple edges? Not needed as we generate all non-path edges which are not consecutive. but in selection phase, if any are duplicate? no because u <v in the loops.

    # Now output sp = P, and mst sum is also P
    print(P, P)
    #print the edges:
    for edge in all_edges:
        print(edge[0], edge[1], edge[2])
    return

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

    11	def parse_comments(func):
    12	    """
    13	    parse function comments
    14	
    15	    First line of comments will be saved as summary, and the rest
    16	    will be saved as description.
    17	    """
    18	    doc = inspect.getdoc(func)
    19	    if doc is None:
    20	        return None, None
    21	    doc = doc.split('\n', 1)
    22	    if len(doc) == 1:
    23	        return doc[0], None
    24	    return doc[0], doc[1].strip()

    27	def parse_request(func):
    28	    """
    29	    get json spec
    30	    """
    31	    data = {}
    32	    if hasattr(func, 'json'):
    33	        data = {
    34	            'content': {
    35	                'application/json': {
    36	                    'schema': {
    37	                        '$ref': f'#/components/schemas/{func.json}'
    38	                    }
    39	                }
    40	            }

    44	
    45	def parse_params(func, params, models):
    46	    """
    47	    get spec for (query, headers, cookies)
    48	    """
    49	    if hasattr(func, 'query'):
    50	        query = models[func.query]
    51	        for name, schema in query['properties'].items():
    52	            params.append({
    53	                'name': name,
    54	                'in': 'query',
    55	                'schema': schema,
    56	                'required': name in query.get('required', []),
    57	            })
    58	
    59	    if hasattr(func, 'headers'):
    60	        headers = models[func.headers]
    61	        for name, schema in headers['properties'].items():
    62	            params.append({
    63	                'name': name,
    64	                'in': 'header',
    65	                'schema': schema,
    66	                'required': name in headers.get('required', []),
    67	            })
    68	
    69	    if hasattr(func, 'cookies'):
    70	        cookies = models[func.cookies]
    71	        for name, schema in cookies['properties'].items():
    72	            params.append({
    73	                'name': name,
    74	                'in': 'cookie',
    75	                'schema': schema,
    76	                'required': name in cookies.get('required', []),
    77	            })
    78	
    79	    return params
    80	
    81	
    82	def parse_resp(func):
    83	    """
    84	    get the response spec
    85	
    86	    If this function does not have explicit ``resp`` but have other models,
    87	    a ``422 Validation Error`` will be append to the response spec. Since
    88	    this may be triggered in the validation step.
    89	    """
    90	    responses = {}
    91	    if hasattr(func, 'resp'):
    92	        responses = func.resp.generate_spec()
    93	
    94	    if '422' not in responses and has_model(func):
    95	        responses['422'] = {'description': 'Validation Error'}
    96	
    97	    return responses
    98	
    99	
   100	def has_model(func):
   101	    """
   102	    return True if this function have ``pydantic.BaseModel``
   103	    """
   104	    if any(hasattr(func, x) for x in ('query', 'json', 'headers')):
   105	        return True
   106	
   107	    if hasattr(func, 'resp') and func.resp.has_model():
   108	        return True
   109	
   110	    return False
   111	
   112	
   113	def parse_code(http_code):
   114	    """
   115	    get the code of this HTTP status
   116	
   117	    :param str http_code: format like ``HTTP_200``
   118	    """
   119	    match = HTTP_CODE.match(http_code)
   120	    if not match:
   121	        return None
   122	    return match.group('code')
   123	
   124	
   125	def parse_name(func):
   126	    """
   127	    the func can be
   128	
   129	        * undecorated functions
   130	        * decorated functions
   131	        * decorated class methods
   132	    """
   133	    return func.__name__
   134	
   135	
   136	def default_before_handler(req, resp, req_validation_error, instance):
   137	    """
   138	    default handler called before the endpoint function after the request validation
   139	
   140	    :param req: request provided by the web framework
   141	    :param resp: response generated by SpecTree that will be returned
   142	        if the validation error is not None
   143	    :param req_validation_error: request validation error
   144	    :param instance: class instance if the endpoint function is a class method
   145	    """
   146	    if req_validation_error:
   147	        logger.info(
   148	            '422 Validation Error',
   149	            extra={
   150	                'spectree_model': req_validation_error.model.__name__,
   151	                'spectree_validation': req_validation_error.errors(),
   152	            },
   153	        )
   154	
   155	
   156	def default_after_handler(req, resp, resp_validation_error, instance):
   157	    """
   158	    default handler called after the response validation
   159

   <#sep#>You are tasked with developing a function to classify the sentiment of tweets. Your function should categorize each tweet as either "positive", "negative", or "neutral" based on its sentiment. You are allowed to use any pre-trained sentiment analysis models or libraries available in Python, such as TextBlob, VADER, or others.

**Input:**
- A single string representing a tweet.

**Output:**
- A string indicating the sentiment of the tweet: "positive", "negative", or "neutral".

**Sample Input:**
```
"Absolutely loved the new coffee blend! 🍻☕ #coffee #love"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
positive
```

```python
from textblob import TextBlob

def classify_tweet(tweet):
    """
    Classifies the sentiment of a given tweet as 'positive', 'negative', or 'neutral'.
    
    Parameters:
    tweet (str): The tweet to be analyzed.
    
    Returns:
    str: The sentiment category of the tweet.
    """
    blob = TextBlob(tweet)
    polarity = blob.sentiment.polarity
    
    if polarity > 0:
        return "positive"
    elif polarity < 0:
        return "negative"
    else:
        return "neutral"

```python
import sys
import math

def is_prime(x):
    if x < 2:
        return False
    if x == 2:
        return True
    if x % 2 == 0:
        return False
    for i in range(3, int(math.sqrt(x)) + 1, 2):
        if x % i == 0:
            return False
    return True

def next_prime(x):
    candidate = x if x % 2 != 0 else x + 1
    while True:
        if is_prime(candidate):
            return candidate
        candidate += 2

def main():
    n, m = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    # Find the smallest prime P ≥ n-1
    current = n - 1
    P = next_prime(current)
    # Construct path edges
    path_edges = []
    for i in range(n - 1):
        if i < n - 2:
            weight = 1
        else:
            weight = P - (n - 2)
        path_edges.append((i + 1, i + 2, weight))
    # Calculate remaining edges needed
    edges_needed = m - (n - 1)
    # Generate non-path edges (all possible u, v where |u - v| > 1)
    non_path_edges = []
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        for v in range(u + 2, n + 1):
            non_path_edges.append((u, v, 10**9))
    selected_edges = []
    for edge in non_path_edges:
        if len(selected_edges) < edges_needed:
            selected_edges.append(edge)
        else:
            break
    all_edges = path_edges + selected_edges
    assert len(all_edges) == m
    print(P, P)
    for edge in all_edges:
        print(edge[0], edge[1], edge[2])

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

<#sep#>You are given a string `sentence` representing a sentence. Your task is to write a Python function `sentence_to_pos(sentence)` that returns a list of tuples. Each tuple should contain a word from the sentence and its corresponding part of speech tag. Use the Natural Language Toolkit (nltk) library to accomplish this task. Assume that the necessary NLTK data files are already downloaded.

**Sample Input:**
```
"John is walking in the park."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[('John', 'NNP'), ('is', 'VBZ'), ('walking', 'VBG'), ('in', 'IN'), ('the', 'DT'), ('park', 'NN'), ('.', '.')]
```

```python
import nltk
from nltk import pos_tag
from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize

def sentence_to_pos(sentence):
    """
    Takes a sentence and returns a list of tuples containing words and their part of speech tags.

    :param sentence: A string representing a sentence.
    :return: A list of tuples, where each tuple contains a word and its part of speech tag.
    """
    words = word_tokenize(sentence)
    pos_tags = pos_tag(words)
    return pos_tags

# Example usage
sentence = "John is walking in the park."
result = sentence_to_pos(sentence)
print(result)

Phase 4. TEST CREATION: before<#sep#>You are given a list of tuples, where each tuple consists of a string and an integer. Your task is to sort this list in descending order based on the integer value in each tuple. Implement a function `sort_tuples_by_number_desc` that takes a list of tuples as input and returns a new list sorted according to the specified criteria.

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains a string and an integer.
- The list can contain up to 1000 tuples.
- The integer values can range from -1000 to 1000.

**Output:**
- A new list of tuples sorted in descending order by the integer value.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[("apple", 2), ("banana", 5), ("cherry", 1)]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[("banana", 5), ("apple", 2), ("cherry", 1)]
```

```python
def sort_tuples_by_number_desc(tuples_list):
    """
    Sorts a list of tuples in descending order based on the second item of each tuple.

    Parameters:
    tuples_list (list of tuples): A list where each tuple contains a string and an integer.

    Returns:
    list of tuples: A new list of tuples sorted in descending order by the integer.
    """
    return sorted(tuples_list, key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True)

# Example usage:
tuples_list = [("apple", 2), ("banana", 5), ("cherry", 1)]
sorted_tuples = sort_tuples_by_number_desc(tuples_list)
print(sorted_tuples)  # Output: [('banana', 5), ('apple', 2), ('cherry', 1)]

     1	# Spectree
     2	
     3	
     4	[![GitHub Actions](https://github.com/0b01001001/spectree/workflows/Python%20package/badge.svg)](https://github.com/0b01001001/spectree/actions)
     5	[![pypi](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/spectree.svg)](https://pypi.python.org/pypi/spectree)
     6	[![versions](https://img.shields.io/pypi/pyversions/spectree.svg)](https://github.com/0b01001001/spectree)
     7	[![Language grade: Python](https://img.shields.io/lgtm/grade/python/g/0b01001001/spectree.svg?logo=lgtm&logoWidth=18)](https://lgtm.com/projects/g/0b01001001/spectree/context:python)
     8	[![Documentation Status](https://readthedocs.org/projects/spectree/badge/?version=latest)](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest/?badge=latest)
     9	
    10	Yet another library to generate OpenAPI document and validate request & response with Python annotations.
    11	
    12	## Features
    13	
    14	* Less boilerplate code, only annotations, no need for YAML :sparkles:
    15	* Generate API document with [Redoc UI](https://github.com/Redocly/redoc) or [Swagger UI](https://github.com/swagger-api/swagger-ui) :yum:
    16	* Validate query, JSON data, response data with [pydantic](https://github.com/samuelcolvin/pydantic/) :wink:
    17	* Current support:
    18	  * Flask [demo](#flask)
    19	  * Falcon [demo](#falcon)
    20	  * Starlette [demo](#starlette)
    21	
    22	## Quick Start
    23	
    24	install with pip: `pip install spectree`
    25	
    26	### Examples
    27	
    28	Check the [examples](/examples) folder.
    29	
    30	* [flask example](/examples/flask_demo.py)
    31	* [falcon example with logging when validation failed](/examples/falcon_demo.py)
    32	* [starlette example](examples/starlette_demo.py)

Define your data structure used in (query, json, headers, cookies, resp)<#sep#>You are given a JSON file named `books.json` that contains an array of book objects, each with the following properties: `Title`, `Author`, and `ISBN`. Your task is to write a program that adds a new book to this JSON file. The new book should have the following properties: `Title: "The Great Gatsby"`, `Author: "F. Scott Fitzgerald"`, `ISBN: "9780743273565"`. Your program should handle potential errors such as file not found, invalid JSON format, and IO errors during file operations.

**Sample Input:**
```json
[
    {"Title": "1984", "Author": "George Orwell", "ISBN": "9780451524935"},
    {"Title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "Author": "Harper Lee", "ISBN": "9780060935467"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```json
[
    {"Title": "1984", "Author": "George Orwell", "ISBN": "9780451524935"},
    {"Title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "Author": "Harper Lee", "ISBN": "9780060935467"},
    {"Title": "The Great Gatsby", "Author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "ISBN": "9780743273565"}
]
```

```python
import json
import os

def read_books_file(file_path):
    """
    Reads a JSON file and returns the list of books.
    
    Args:
        file_path (str): The path to the JSON file.
    
    Returns:
        list: A list of book dictionaries.
    
    Raises:
        FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
        ValueError: If the file does not contain valid JSON.
    """
    if not os.path.isfile(file_path):
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"File {file_path} does not exist.")
    with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
        try:
            books = json.load(file)
        except json.JSONDecodeError:
            raise ValueError("File does not contain valid JSON.")
    return books

def write_books_file(file_path, books):
    """
    Writes a list of books to a JSON file.
    
    Args:
        file_path (str): The path to the JSON file.
        books (list): A list of book dictionaries.
    
    Raises:
        IOError: If an error occurs during file writing.
    """
    with open(file_path, 'w') as file:
        json.dump(books, file, indent=4)

def add_new_book(file_path, new_book):
    """
    Adds a new book to the JSON file.
    
    Args:
        file_path (str): The path to the JSON file.
        new_book (dict): The new book dictionary to add.
    """
    try:
        books = read_books_file(file_path)
    except (FileNotFoundError, ValueError) as e:
        print(f"Error reading the file: {e}. Please check the file and try again.")
        return

    books.append(new_book)

    try:
        write_books_file(file_path, books)
    except IOError as e:
        print(f"Error writing to the file: {e}. Please try again.")
        return

    print("The new book was successfully added to the file.")

# Using the function to add a new book:
file_path = 'books.json'  # Adjust the file path as necessary
new_book = {'Title': 'The Great Gatsby', 'Author': 'F. Scott Fitzgerald', 'ISBN': '9780743273565'}
add_new_book(file_path, new_book)

    25	
    26	### Examples
    27	
    28	Check the [examples](/examples) folder.
    29	
    30	* [flask example](/examples/flask_demo.py)
    31	* [falcon example with logging when validation failed](/examples/falcon_demo.py)
    32	* [starlette example](examples/starlette_demo.py)
    33	
    34	### Step by Step
    35	
    36	1. Define your data structure used in (query, json, headers, cookies, resp) with `pydantic.BaseModel`
    37	2. create `spectree.SpecTree` instance with the web framework name you are using, like `api = SpecTree('flask')`
    38	3. `api.validate` decorate the route with
    39	   * `query`
    40	   * `json`
    41	   * `headers`
    42	   * `cookies`
    43	   * `resp`
    44	   * `tags`
    45	4. access these data with `context(query, json, headers, cookies)` (of course, you can access these from the original place where the framework offered)
    46	   * flask: `request.context`
    47	   * falcon: `req.context`
    48	   * starlette: `request.context`
    49	5. register to the web application `api.register(app)`
    50	6. check the document at URL location `/apidoc/redoc` or `/apidoc/swagger`
    51	
    52	If the request doesn't pass the validation, it will return a 422 with JSON error message(ctx, loc, msg, type).
    53	
    54	## How To
    55	
    56	> How to add summary and description to endpoints?
    57	
    58	Just add docs to the endpoint function. The 1st line is the summary, and the rest is the description for this endpoint.
    59	
    60	> How to add description to parameters?
    61	
    62	Check the [pydantic](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/schema/) document about description in `Field`.
    63	
    64	> Any config I can change?
    65	
    66	Of course. Check the [config](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest/config.html) document.
    67	
    68	You can update the config when init the spectree like: 
    69	
    70	```py
    71	SpecTree('flask', title='Demo API', version='v1.0', path='doc')
    72	```
    73	
    74	> What is `Response` and how to use it?
    75	
    76	To build a response for the endpoint, you need to declare the status code with format `HTTP_{code}` and corresponding data (optional).
    77	
    78	```py
    79	Response(HTTP_200=None, HTTP_403=ForbidModel)
    80	Response('HTTP_200') # equals to Response(HTTP_200=None)
    81	```
    82	
    83	> What should I return when I'm using the library?
    84	
    85	No need to change anything. Just return what the framework required.
    86	
    87	> How to logging when the validation failed?
    88	
    89	Validation errors are logged with INFO level. Details are passed into `extra`. Check the [falcon example](examples/falcon_demo.py) for details.
    90	
    91	> How can I write a customized plugin for another backend framework?
    92	
    93	Inherit `spectree.plugins.base.BasePlugin` and implement the functions you need. After that, init like `api = SpecTree(backend=MyCustomizedPlugin)`.
    94	
    95	> How can I change the response when there is a validation error? Can I record some metrics?
    96	
    97	This library provides `before` and `after` hooks to do these. Check the [doc](https://spectree.readthedocs.io/en/latest) or the [test case](tests/test_plugin_flask.py). You can change the handlers for SpecTree or for a specific endpoint validation.
    98	
    99	## Demo
   100	
   101	Try it with `http post :8000/api/user name=alice age=18`. (if you are using `httpie`)
   102	
   103	### Flask
   104	
   105	```py
   106	from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
   107	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   108	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   109	
   110	
   111	class Profile(BaseModel):
   112	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40) # Constrained Str
   113	    age: int = Field(
   114	        ...,
   115	        gt=0,
   116	        lt=150,
   117	        description='user age(Human)'
   118	    )
   119	
   120	    class Config:
   121	        schema_extra = {
   122	            # provide an example
   123	            'example': {
   124	                'name': 'very_important_user',
   125	                'age': 42,
   126	            }
   127	        }
   128	
   129	
   130	class Message(BaseModel):
   131	    text: str
   132	
   133	
   134	app = Flask(__name__)
   135	api = SpecTree('flask')
   136	
   137	
   138	@app.route('/api/user', methods=['POST'])
   139	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   140	def user_profile():
   141	    """
   142	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   143	
   144	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   145	    """
   146	    print(request.context.json) # or `request.json`
   147	    return jsonify(text='it works')
   148	
   149	
   150	if __name__ == "__main__":
   151	    api.register(app) # if you don't register in api init step
   152	    app.run(port=8000)
   153	
   154	```
   155	
   156	### Falcon
   157	
   158	```py
   159	import falcon
   160	from wsgiref import simple_server
   161	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   162	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   163	
   164	
   165	class Profile(BaseModel):
   166	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   167	    age: int = Field(
   168	        ...,
   169	        gt=0,
   170	        lt=150,
   171	        description='user age(Human)'
   172	    )
   173	
   174	
   175	class Message(BaseModel):
   176	    text: str
   177	
   178	
   179	api = SpecTree('falcon')
   180	
   181	
   182	class UserProfile:
   183	    @api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   184	    def on_post(self, req, resp):
   185	        """
   186	        verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   187	
   188	        user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   189	        """
   190	        print(req.context.json)  # or `req.media`
   191	        resp.media = {'text': 'it works'}
   192	
   193	
   194	if __name__ == "__main__":
   195	    app = falcon.API()
   196	    app.add_route('/api/user', UserProfile())
   197	    api.register(app)
   198	
   199	    httpd = simple_server.make_server('localhost', 8000, app)
   200	    httpd.serve_forever()
   201	
   202	```
   203<#sep#>You are given a DNA sequence and a set of primers. Your task is to determine if the primers are complementary to the DNA sequence. The DNA sequence should be treated as a single strand, and the primers should be treated as complementary strands. For each primer, output a tuple containing the primer and a boolean indicating whether it is complementary to the DNA sequence.

**Input:**
- The first line contains a string `dna_sequence` (1 ≤ |dna_sequence| ≤ 1000) representing the DNA sequence.
- The second line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 100) representing the number of primers.
- The next `n` lines each contain a string `primer` (1 ≤ |primer| ≤ 100) representing a primer.

**Output:**
- Output `n` lines, each containing a primer and a boolean value (True or False) indicating whether the primer is complementary to the DNA sequence.

**Sample Input:**
```
ATGGCTAGCTAAG
2
ATCG
AGCT
```

**Sample Output:**
```
('ATCG', False)
('AGCT', True)
```

```python
def is_complementary(dna_sequence, primer_set):
    """
    Determines if each primer in primer_set is complementary to the dna_sequence.
    
    :param dna_sequence: A string representing the DNA sequence.
    :param primer_set: A list of strings representing the primers.
    :return: A list of tuples, each containing a primer and a boolean indicating if it is complementary.
    """
    def reverse_string(s):
        """
        Reverses the given string.
        
        :param s: The string to be reversed.
        :return: The reversed string.
        """
        return s[::-1]
    
    def complement(dna):
        """
        Returns the complement of the given DNA strand.
        
        :param dna: A string representing the DNA strand.
        :return: A string representing the complement of the DNA strand.
        """
        complement = {'A': 'T', 'T': 'A', 'C': 'G', 'G': 'C'}
        return ''.join([complement[base] for base in dna])
    
    results = []
    for primer in primer_set:
        complement_primer = complement(reverse_string(primer))
        is_comp = complement_primer in dna_sequence
        results.append((primer, is_comp))
    return results

# Example usage
dna_sequence = "ATGGCTAGCTAAG"
primer_set = ["ATCG", "AGCT"]
result = is_complementary(dna_sequence, primer_set)
for res in result:
    print(res)

   206	```py
   207	import uvicorn
   208	from starlette.applications import Starlette
   209	from starlette.routing import Route, Mount
   210	from starlette.responses import JSONResponse
   211	from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, constr
   212	from spectree import SpecTree, Response
   213	
   214	
   215	class Profile(BaseModel):
   216	    name: constr(min_length=2, max_length=40)  # Constrained Str
   217	    age: int = Field(
   218	        ...,
   219	        gt=0,
   220	        lt=150,
   221	        description='user age(Human)'
   222	    )
   223	
   224	
   225	class Message(BaseModel):
   226	    text: str
   227	
   228	
   229	api = SpecTree('starlette')
   230	
   231	
   232	@api.validate(json=Profile, resp=Response(HTTP_200=Message, HTTP_403=None), tags=['api'])
   233	async def user_profile(request):
   234	    """
   235	    verify user profile (summary of this endpoint)
   236	
   237	    user's name, user's age, ... (long description)
   238	    """
   239	    print(request.context.json)  # or await request.json()
   240	    return JSONResponse({'text': 'it works'})
   241	
   242	
   243	if __name__ == "__main__":
   244	    app = Starlette(routes=[
   245	        Mount('api', routes=[
   246	            Route('/user', user_profile, methods=['POST']),
   247	        ])
   248	    ])
   249	    api.register(app)
   250	
   251	    uvicorn.run(app)
   252	
   253	```

   255	## FAQ
   256	
   257	> ValidationError: missing field for headers
   258	
   259	The HTTP headers' keys in Flask are capitalized, in Falcon are upper cases, in Starlette are lower cases.
   260	You can use [`pydantic.root_validators(pre=True)`](https://pydantic-docs.helpmanual.io/usage/validators/#root-validators) to change all the keys into lower cases or upper cases.
   261	
   262	> ValidationError: value is not a valid list for query
   263	
   264	Since there is no standard for HTTP query with multiple values, it's hard to find the way to handle this for different web frameworks. So I suggest not to use list type in query until I find a suitable way to fix it.

benchmark: 5<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a function to parse HTML content and extract the text from all `<p>` tags. Your function should accept a string containing HTML and return a list of strings, where each string represents the text content of a `<p>` tag. Use the BeautifulSoup library for parsing. 

**Input:**
- A single string `html_string` representing the HTML content.

**Output:**
- A list of strings, each containing the text from a `<p>` tag in the order they appear in the HTML.

**Sample Input:**
```html
<html><body><p>This is the first paragraph.</p><p>This is the second paragraph.</p></body></html>
```

**Sample Output:**
```
['This is the first paragraph.', 'This is the second paragraph.']
```

```python
from bs4 import BeautifulSoup

def extract_paragraph_text(html_string):
    """
    Extracts and returns the text content of all <p> tags from the given HTML string.

    Parameters:
    html_string (str): A string containing HTML content.

    Returns:
    list: A list of strings, each representing the text content of a <p> tag.
    """
    soup = BeautifulSoup(html_string, 'html.parser')
    paragraphs = soup.find_all('p')
    return [p.get_text() for p in paragraphs]

# Example usage:
html_string = "<html><body><p>This is the first paragraph.</p><p>This is the second paragraph.</p></body></html>"
print(extract_paragraph_text(html_string))

Requirement already satisfied: importlib-metadata>=3.6.0 in /opt/conda/envs/testbed<#sep#>You are given a list of strings. Your task is to implement a function that returns a dictionary with each unique string as a key and the number of times it appears in the list as the value. The function should support both case-sensitive and case-insensitive counting based on a boolean parameter.

**Input:**
- A list of strings `strings` (1 ≤ len(strings) ≤ 10^4).
- A boolean `case_sensitive` indicating whether the counting should be case-sensitive.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where keys are unique strings and values are their respective counts.

**Sample Input 1:**
```python
["Apple", "apple", "banana", "Banana", "APPLE"], False
```

**Sample Output 1:**
```python
{'apple': 3, 'banana': 2}
```

**Sample Input 2:**
```python
["Apple", "apple", "banana", "Banana", "APPLE"], True
```

**Sample Output 2:**
```python
{'Apple': 1, 'apple': 1, 'banana': 1, 'Banana': 1, 'APPLE': 1}
```

```python
def count_strings(strings, case_sensitive=False):
    """
    Counts the occurrences of unique strings in a given list.
    
    Parameters:
    - strings (list): A list of strings.
    - case_sensitive (bool): A flag to determine if the string matching should be case-sensitive.
    
    Returns:
    - dict: A dictionary with strings as keys and their counts as values.
    """
    if not case_sensitive:
        strings = [s.lower() for s in strings]
    
    string_counts = {}
    for string in strings:
        if string in string_counts:
            string_counts[string] += 1
        else:
            string_counts[string] = 1
            
    return string_counts

**Final Answer**
```python
import sys
def replace_terrain(rows):
    h = len(rows)
    w = len(rows[0]) if h else 0
    moles = []
    for i in range(h):
        s = rows[i]
        for j in range(len(s)-1):
            if s[j] == '/' and s[j+1] == '\\':
                moles.append( (i,j) )
    top_pad = max( [ (m[0] + 3 - (h -1 - m[0])) for m in moles ], default=0)
    new_h = h + top_pad
    new_rows = [' '*w]*(new_h)
    for i in range(h):
        new_rows[i + top_pad] = rows[i]
    for i,j in moles:
        r = i + top_pad
        for d in range(1,5):
            if r - d >=0:
                new_rows[r - d] = new_rows[r -d][:j -d+1] + '/' + new_rows[r -d][j -d+2:]
                if j+1+d < w:
                    new_rows[r-d] = new_rows[r-d][:j+1+d] + '\\' + new_rows[r-d][j+2+d:]
    return '\n'.join(new_rows)

print(replace_terrain([line.rstrip() for line in sys.stdin]))
```
</think>

The task requires converting molehills into mountains in a given terrain string. A molehill is identified as an adjacent pair of columns where the left is a '/' (rising slope) and the right is a '\' (falling slope), with the shortest side exactly one column wide. The mountain must be centered in the same column as the molehill, potentially requiring additional rows at the top of the string.

### Approach
1. **Identify Molehills**: Traverse the terrain rows to detect molehills, defined by consecutive '/' and '\' in adjacent columns.
2. **Calculate Padding**: Determine the required additional rows at the top to accommodate the tallest possible mountains. Each mountain requires 4 rows above the molehill.
3. **Expand Terrain**: Extend the terrain to include the required padding, initially filled with spaces.
4. **Construct Mountains**: For each molehill, replace the '/' and '\' with the slopes of a mountain expanding upwards over 4 rows, ensuring the mountain's slopes are centered.

### Solution Code

    path, parameters = self<#sep#>You are tasked with processing a CSV file that contains product information. Each row in the CSV file includes the product name, price, and category. Your goal is to implement a function that reads this CSV file and constructs a dictionary mapping each product name to its price. The function should gracefully handle cases where the CSV file might contain missing or invalid data.

**Input:**
- A string representing the filename of the CSV file. The CSV file has the following format:
  - The first row is a header row and should be ignored.
  - Each subsequent row contains three comma-separated values: product name, price, and category.
  - The product name and price fields may be missing or contain invalid data (e.g., non-numeric price).

**Output:**
- A dictionary where the keys are product names (strings) and the values are their respective prices (floats).

**Sample Input:**
```
product_name,price,category
Laptop,999.99,Electronics
Smartphone,,Electronics
Tablet,499.99,Electronics
Monitor,199.99,Electronics
Chair,89.99,Furniture
Desk,,Furniture
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'Laptop': 999.99,
    'Tablet': 499.99,
    'Monitor': 199.99,
    'Chair': 89.99
}
```

```python
import csv

def read_products(csv_filename):
    """
    Reads a CSV file and returns a dictionary mapping product names to their prices.
    
    Parameters:
    csv_filename (str): The filename of the CSV file to read.
    
    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary where keys are product names and values are their respective prices.
    """
    products = {}
    
    try:
        with open(csv_filename, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            csv_reader = csv.reader(file)
            next(csv_reader, None)  # Skip header row
            
            for row in csv_reader:
                if len(row) < 3:
                    continue
                
                product_name, price, _ = row[:3]
                
                if not product_name or not price:
                    continue
                
                try:
                    price = float(price)
                except ValueError:
                    continue
                
                products[product_name] = price
            
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file '{csv_filename}' was not found.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
    
    return products

But maybe there's a property that AR/AP= AQ/AP=... but since Q=R, then AP is from A to P, and AR is from A to R=Q, which is a point further along the line AP beyond the circumcircle. Wait,<#sep#>import sys

def replace_terrain(rows):
    h = len(rows)
    if h == 0:
        return ""
    w = len(rows[0])
    moles = []
    # Find molehill positions
    for i in range(h):
        current = rows[i]
        for j in range(len(current)-1):
            if current[j] == '/' and current[j+1] == '\\':
                moles.append((i, j))
    # Calculate needed top padding
    top_pad = 0
    for (y, x) in moles:
        required = (y + 3) - (h - 1 - y)
        if required > top_pad:
            top_pad = required
    new_h = h + top_pad
    new_rows = [' ' * w for _ in range(new_h)]
    # Copy original terrain into new rows with padding
    for i in range(h):
        new_rows[i + top_pad] = rows[i]
    # Replace molehills with mountains
    for (y, x) in moles:
        r = y + top_pad
        for d in range(1,5):
            if r - d >= 0:
                # Left side slope
                if x - d +1 >=0:
                    # The segment to replace on the left
                    prefix = new_rows[r-d][:x - d +1]
                    new_part = prefix + '/' + new_rows[r-d][x -d +2:]
                    new_rows[r-d] = new_part[:x -d +2 +1] + new_part[x -d +2 +1:]  # adjusted
                # Ensure within bounds
                if x + 1 + d < w:
                    end = x+1+d
                    right_part = '\\' + new_rows[r-d][end+1:]
                    new_part = new_rows[r-d][:end] + right_part
                    new_rows[r-d] = new_part
    return '\n'.join(new_rows)

# Read input lines and process
if __name__ == "__main__":
    input_lines = [line.rstrip() for line in sys.stdin]
    print(replace_terrain(input_lines))
```

### Explanation
1. **Identify Molehills**: By iterating over each row and checking for the pattern '/' followed by '\', we identify the positions of molehills.
2. **Padding Calculation**: Each mountain requires 4 rows above the molehill. The required padding is determined based on the deepest molehill in the terrain.
3. **Expand Terrain**: The original rows are shifted downwards by the calculated padding to accommodate new rows at the top.
4. **Construct Mountains**: For each molehill, we expand the slopes upwards over four rows, ensuring the slopes are properly placed using the detected positions and padding.

This approach ensures that each molehill is correctly replaced by a mountain of the required size while preserving the rest of the terrain structure.<#sep#> _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _

/testbed/spectree/plugins/falcon_plugin<#sep#>You are given a positive integer `n` greater than 1. Your task is to write a function `find_prime_factors` that returns a list of all prime factors of `n` in ascending order.

**Input:**
- An integer `n` (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^6)

**Output:**
- A list of integers representing the prime factors of `n` in ascending order.

**Sample Input:**
```
60
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[2, 2, 3, 5]
```

```python
def find_prime_factors(n):
    """
    Return the list of prime factors of the integer n, in ascending order.
    
    Parameters:
    n (int): A positive integer greater than 1.
    
    Returns:
    list: A list of integers representing the prime factors of n in ascending order.
    """
    prime_factors = []
    # Check for the number of 2s that divide n
    while n % 2 == 0:
        prime_factors.append(2)
        n //= 2
    # n must be odd at this point, so a skip of 2 (i.e., i = i + 2) can be used
    for i in range(3, int(n**0.5) + 1, 2):
        # While i divides n, append i and divide n
        while n % i == 0:
            prime_factors.append(i)
            n //= i
    # If n is a prime number greater than 2, then it will not be reduced to 1 by above two steps
    if n > 2:
        prime_factors.append(n)
    return prime_factors

</think>To solve the problem, we need to find the value of \(\frac{AR}{AP}\) where \(R\) is the second intersection point of \(AP\) with the circumcircle of \(\triangle ABC\). We start by determining the coordinates of points \(A\), \(B\), and \(C\), and then proceed to find the coordinates of the feet of the altitudes \(X\) and \(Y\), the intersection point \(P\), and finally the points \(Q\) and \(R\).

1. **Coordinates of Points**:
   - Place \(B\) at \((0, 0)\) and \(C\) at \((14, 0)\).
   - Let \(A\) be at \((x, y)\). Using the distances \(AB = 13\) and \(AC = 15\), we solve the system of equations:
     \[
     x^2 + y^2 = 169
     \]
     \[
     (x-14)^2 + y^2 = 225
     \]
     Subtracting the first equation from the second, we get:
     \[
     (x-14)^2 - x^2 = 56 \implies -28x + 196 = 56 \implies x = 5
     \]
     Substituting \(x = 5\) into \(x^2 + y^2 = 169\), we get:
     \[
     25 + y^2 = 169 \implies y^2 = 144 \implies y = 12
     \]
     So, \(A\) is at \((5, 12)\).

2. **Feet of the Altitudes**:
   - The foot \(X\) of the altitude from \(B\) to \(AC\) is found by solving the intersection of the line \(AC\) (with slope \(-\frac{4}{3}\)) and the altitude from \(B\) (with slope \(\frac{3}{4}\)):
     \[
     y = \frac{3}{4}x \quad \text{and} \quad y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{56}{3}
     \]
     Solving these, we get:
     \[
     \frac{3}{4}x = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{56}{3} \implies \frac{25}{12}x = \frac{56}{3} \implies x = \frac{224}{25}, \quad y = \frac{168}{25}
     \]
     So, \(X\) is at \(\left( \frac{224}{25}, \frac{168}{25} \right)\).
   - The foot \(Y\) of the altitude from \(C\) to \(AB\) is found similarly:
     \[
     y = \frac{12}{5}x \quad \text{and} \quad y = -\frac{5}{12}(x-14)
     \]
     Solving these, we get:
     \[
     \frac{12}{5}x = -\frac{5}{12}x + \frac{35}{6} \implies \frac{169}{60}x = \frac{35}{6} \implies x = \frac{350}{169}, \quad y = \frac{840}{169}
     \]
     So, \(Y\) is at \(\left( \frac{350}{169}, \frac{840}{169} \right)\).

3. **Intersection Point \(P\)**:
   - The line \(XY\) intersects \(BC\) at \(P\). The equation of \(XY\) is:
     \[
     y = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{168}{25} - \frac{16}{63} \cdot \frac{224}{25} = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{168 \cdot 63 - 16 \cdot 224}{25 \cdot 63} = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{10584 - 3584}{1575} = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{7000}{1575} = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{280}{63}
     \]
     Setting \(y = 0\), we get:
     \[
     0 = \frac{16}{63}x + \frac{280}{63} \implies x = -17.5
     \]
     So, \(P\) is at \((-17.5, 0)\).

4. **Circumcircles and Intersection \(Q\)**:

     \[
     Q = \left( \frac{455}{289}, \frac{2940}{289} \right)
     \]

The second intersection point \(R\) is the same as \(Q<#sep#>human: Chaim Goodman-Strauss, Craig Kaplan, Joseph Myers and David Smith found the following simple (both objectively and subjectively) polygon that tiles the plane, but only aperiodically:

Indeed they found a one-parameter family of such aperiodic monotiles or "einsteins". The edges of all tiles in this family meet at 90° or 120° and are made out of two distinct lengths:

Let \$a\$ and \$b\$ be nonnegative real numbers and define the following turtle graphics commands:

\$A,B\$: move forwards by \$a\$ or \$b\$ respectively (blue and purple edges in the image above)
\$L,R\$: turn left or right by 90° respectively
\$S,T\$: turn left or right by 60° respectively

Then the aperiodic tile \$T(a,b)\$ associated with \$a\$ and \$b\$ is traced by the command sequence
$$ASAR\ BSBR\ ATAL\ BTBR\ ATAL\ BTBBTBR$$
It is clear that \$T(a,b)\$ is similar to \$T(ka,kb)\$ for any scaling constant \$k\$, so we can reduce the number of parameters to one by defining $$T(p)=T(p,1-p)\qquad0\le p\le1$$
The tile in this challenge's first picture – the "hat" of the GKMS paper – is \$T\left(\frac{\sqrt3}{\sqrt3+1}\right)\$. \$T(0)\$, \$T(1)\$ and \$T(1/2)\$ admit periodic tilings, but the first two of these exceptions are polyiamonds and play a central role in the proof that \$T(p)\$ for all other \$p\in[0,1]\$ is aperiodic.
Task

The polygon may be drawn in any orientation and may be flipped from the schematic above (though of course the aspect ratio must not be changed). It may be filled with any colour or pattern and may optionally be outlined with any colour, but the polygon's boundary must be clear.
Raster images must be at least 400 pixels wide, and an error of 2 pixels/1% is allowed.

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year of the car.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year

    def start_engine(self):
        """
        Prints a message indicating the engine is starting.
        """
        print(f"The engine of the {self.year} {self.make} {self.model} is starting.")

    def stop_engine(self):
        """
        Prints a message indicating the engine is stopping.
        """
        print(f"The engine of the {self.year} {self.make} {self.model} is stopping.")

class ElectricCar(Car):
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, battery_size):
        """
        Initializes a new ElectricCar instance.

        :param make: The make of the electric car.
        :param model: The model of the electric car.
        :param year: The year of the electric car.
        :param battery_size: The battery size of the electric car in kWh.
        """
        super().__init__(make, model, year)
        self.battery_size = battery_size

    def charge_battery(self):
        """
        Prints a message indicating the battery is charging along with the battery size.
        """
        print(f"The battery of the {self.year} {self.make} {self.model} is charging. Battery size: {self.battery_size} kWh.")

    85	                 query=None, json=None, headers=None, cookies=None, resp=None,<#sep#>You are given a credit card number as a string. Your task is to implement a function that validates the credit card number using the Luhn algorithm. The Luhn algorithm is defined as follows:
1. Starting from the rightmost digit (which is the check digit), double the value of every second digit.
2. If doubling a number results in a number greater than 9, subtract 9 from the result.
3. Sum all the digits.
4. If the total modulo 10 is equal to 0, the number is valid.

Write a function `validate_credit_card(number)` that takes a string `number` as input and returns `True` if the number is valid according to the Luhn algorithm, and `False` otherwise.

**Sample Input:**
```
"4539 1488 0343 6467"
"1234 5678 1234 5670"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
True
False
```

```python
def validate_credit_card(number):
    """
    Validates a credit card number using the Luhn algorithm.

    Parameters:
    number (str): The credit card number as a string.

    Returns:
    bool: True if the number is valid, False otherwise.
    """
    # Convert string to a list of integers
    digits = [int(d) for d in str(number) if d.isdigit()]
    
    # Initialize sum
    total_sum = 0
    num_digits = len(digits)
    
    # Iterate over digits right to left
    for i in range(num_digits - 1, -1, -1):
        digit = digits[i]
        # Double every second digit from the right
        if (num_digits - i) % 2 == 0:
            digit *= 2
            # Subtract 9 if doubling results in a number greater than 9
            if digit > 9:
                digit -= 9
        total_sum += digit
    
    # If the total modulo 10 is equal to 0, the number is valid
    return total_sum % 10 == 0
```<#sep#>Simplify $\frac{10a^3}{55a^2}$ when $a=3$.

</think>To simplify the expression \(\frac{10a^3}{55a^2}\) when \(a = 3\), we follow these steps:

1. **Simplify the coefficients:**
   The coefficients in the numerator and the denominator are 10 and 55, respectively. The greatest common divisor (GCD) of 10 and 55 is 5. We divide both the numerator and the denominator by 5:
   \[
   \frac{10}{55} = \frac{10 \div 5}{55 \div 5} = \frac{2}{11}
   \]

2. **Simplify the variables:**
   The variable part in the numerator is \(a^3\) and in the denominator is \(a^2\). When dividing powers with the same base, we subtract the exponents:
   \[
   \frac{a^3}{a^2} = a^{3-2} = a^1 = a
   \]

3. **Combine the simplified parts:**
   Combining the simplified coefficients and variables, we get:
   \[
   \frac{10a^3}{55a^2} = \frac{2a}{11}
   \]

4. **Substitute \(a = 3\):**
   Substitute \(a = 3\) into the simplified expression:
   \[
   \frac{2a}{11} = \frac{2 \cdot 3}{11} = \frac{6}{11}
   \]

Therefore, the simplified expression when \(a = 3\) is:
\[

   108	                    query, json, headers, cookies, resp,
   109	                    before or self.before, after or<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a Python script that continuously monitors a specified directory for the creation of new files. Upon detecting a new file, the script should output the name of the file. The directory to be monitored is provided as input. Use the `watchdog` library to accomplish this task.

**Input:**
- A single line containing the path to the directory to be monitored.

**Output:**
- For each new file detected in the directory, print the full path of the file.

**Sample Input:**
```
/path/to/directory
```

**Sample Output:**
```
/path/to/directory/newfile1.txt
/path/to/directory/newfile2.txt
```

```python
from watchdog.observers import Observer
from watchdog.events import FileSystemEventHandler
import time

class FileHandler(FileSystemEventHandler):
    """
    Handles file system events, specifically the creation of new files.
    """
    def on_created(self, event):
        """
        Called when a new file is created in the monitored directory.
        
        :param event: Event object containing information about the file creation.
        """
        if not event.is_directory:
            print(event.src_path)

def monitor_directory(path):
    """
    Monitors the specified directory for new file creations and prints the path of each new file.
    
    :param path: The path to the directory to be monitored.
    """
    event_handler = FileHandler()
    observer = Observer()
    observer.schedule(event_handler, path, recursive=False)
    observer.start()
    try:
        while True:
            time.sleep(1)
    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        observer.stop()
    observer.join()

if __name__ == '__main__':
    import sys
    directory_path = sys.argv[1]
    monitor_directory(directory_path)

<ROLE>
Your primary role is to assist users by executing commands, modifying code, and solving technical problems effectively. You should be thorough, methodical, and prioritize quality over speed.
* If the user asks a question, like "why is X happening", don't try to fix the problem. Just give an answer to the question.
</ROLE>

<EFFICIENCY>
* Each action you take is somewhat expensive. Wherever possible, combine multiple actions into a single action, e.g. combine multiple bash commands into one, using sed and grep to edit/view multiple files at once.
* When exploring the codebase, use efficient tools like find, grep, and git commands with appropriate filters to minimize unnecessary operations.
</EFFICIENCY>

<FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>
* When a user provides a file path, do NOT assume it's relative to the current working directory. First explore the file system to locate the file before working on it.
* If asked to edit a file, edit the file directly, rather than creating a new file with a different filename.
* For global search-and-replace operations, consider using `sed` instead of opening file editors multiple times.
</FILE_SYSTEM_GUIDELINES>

<CODE_QUALITY>
* Write clean, efficient code with minimal comments. Avoid redundancy in comments: Do not repeat information that can be easily inferred from the code itself.
* When implementing solutions, focus on making the minimal changes needed to solve the problem.
* Before implementing any changes, first thoroughly understand the codebase through exploration.
* If you are adding a lot of code to a function or file, consider splitting the function or file into smaller pieces when appropriate.
</CODE_QUALITY>

<VERSION_CONTROL>
* When configuring git credentials, use "openhands" as the user.name and "openhands@all-hands.dev" as the user.email by default, unless explicitly instructed otherwise.
* Exercise caution with git operations. Do NOT make potentially dangerous changes (e.g., pushing to main, deleting repositories) unless explicitly asked to do so.
* When committing changes, use `git status` to see all modified files, and stage all files necessary for the commit. Use `git commit -a` whenever possible.
* Do NOT commit files that typically shouldn't go into version control (e.g., node_modules/, .env files, build directories, cache files, large binaries) unless explicitly instructed by the user.
* If unsure about committing certain files, check for the presence of .gitignore files or ask the user for clarification.
</VERSION_CONTROL>

<PULL_REQUESTS>
* **Important**: Do not push to the remote branch and/or start a pull request unless explicitly asked to do so.
* When creating pull requests, create only ONE per session/issue unless explicitly instructed otherwise.
* When working with an existing PR, update it with new commits rather than creating additional PRs for the same issue.
* When updating a PR, preserve the original PR title and purpose, updating description only when necessary.
</PULL_REQUESTS>

<PROBLEM_SOLVING_WORKFLOW>
1. EXPLORATION: Thoroughly explore relevant files and understand the context before proposing solutions
2. ANALYSIS: Consider multiple approaches and select the most promising one
3. TESTING:
   * For bug fixes: Create tests to verify issues before implementing fixes
   * For new features: Consider test-driven development when appropriate
   * If the repository lacks testing infrastructure and implementing tests would require extensive setup, consult with the user before investing time in building testing infrastructure
   * If the environment is not set up to run tests, consult with the user first before investing time to install all dependencies

Phase 4. TEST CREATION: before<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Vector2D` that represents a 2-dimensional vector. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Vector Addition**: Given two vectors, return a new vector that is the sum of the two vectors.
2. **Vector Subtraction**: Given two vectors, return a new vector that is the difference between the two vectors.
3. **Dot Product**: Given two vectors, return a scalar that is the dot product of the two vectors.
4. **Magnitude Calculation**: Return the magnitude (length) of the vector.
5. **Normalization**: Return a new vector that is the unit vector (magnitude of 1) in the same direction as the original vector.

**Input**:
- The constructor `__init__` will take two floating-point numbers `x` and `y` as input, representing the components of the vector. Default values are `0.0` for both `x` and `y`.

**Output**:
- The methods `add`, `subtract`, and `normalize` should return a new `Vector2D` object.
- The method `dot_product` should return a floating-point number.
- The method `magnitude` should return a floating-point number.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- `v1 = Vector2D(3, 4)`
- `v2 = Vector2D(1, 2)`
- `v1.add(v2)` should return `Vector2D(4, 6)`
- `v1.subtract(v2)` should return `Vector2D(2, 2)`
- `v1.dot_product(v2)` should return `11`
- `v1.magnitude()` should return `5.0`
- `v1.normalize()` should return `Vector2D(0.6, 0.8)`

```python
import math

class Vector2D:
    def __init__(self, x=0.0, y=0.0):
        """Initialize a 2D vector with components x and y."""
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

    def add(self, other):
        """Return a new vector that is the sum of this vector and another vector."""
        return Vector2D(self.x + other.x, self.y + other.y)

    def subtract(self, other):
        """Return a new vector that is the difference between this vector and another vector."""
        return Vector2D(self.x - other.x, self.y - other.y)

    def dot_product(self, other):
        """Return the dot product of this vector and another vector."""
        return self.x * other.x + self.y * other.y

    def magnitude(self):
        """Return the magnitude (length) of this vector."""
        return math.sqrt(self.x ** 2 + self.y ** 2)

    def normalize(self):
        """Return a new vector that is the unit vector in the same direction as this vector."""
        mag = self.magnitude()
        if mag == 0:
            return Vector2D(0, 0)
        return Vector2D(self.x / mag, self.y / mag)

    def __repr__(self):
        """Return a string representation of the vector."""
        return f"Vector2D({self.x}, {self.y})"

But wait, let me confirm. Since the Fourier transform of a convolution is the product of the Fourier transforms, then yes. Therefore, if \( \widehat{h} = \widehat{f} \widehat{g} \), then h = f * g. Therefore, h(x) is the convolution of f(x) and g(x). 

So the functions are:

\( f(x) = \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|} \), since its Fourier transform is \( \frac{1}{1 + \omega^2} \).

\( g(x) = \frac{\sin x}{\pi x} \), since its Fourier transform is the rectangular function rect(ω/2), which is 1 for |ω| < 1 and 0 otherwise.

Therefore, h(x) is the convolution of these two functions. So the answer would be that f(x) is \( \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|} \), g(x) is \( \frac{\sin x}{\pi x} \), and h(x) is their convolution.

But wait, let me check if the rectangular function's Fourier transform is indeed that. Let me recheck the inverse transform. The rectangular function in the frequency domain, \(\widehat{g}(\omega) = \text{rect}(\omega/(2))\), which is 1 when |ω| ≤1 and 0 otherwise. The inverse Fourier transform is \( \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-1}^{1} e^{i \omega x} d\omega \). Let's compute that:

Integral of e^{i ω x} from -1 to 1 is [e^{i x}/(i x) - e^{-i x}/(i x)] = (2 sin x)/x. Therefore, the inverse transform is (1/(2π)) * (2 sin x)/x = (sin x)/(π x). Yes, so that's correct. So g(x) = sin x/(π x).

Similarly, the Fourier transform of f(x) = (1/2) e^{-|x|} is 1/(1 + ω²), which matches.

Therefore, the product of 1/(1 + ω²) and rect(ω/2) gives us the given \(\widehat{h}(\omega)\). Therefore, h(x) = (f * g)(x) = \int_{-\infty}^\infty (1/2) e^{-|t|} * (sin(x - t)/(π (x - t))) dt. So that's the convolution.

Alternatively, written as h(x) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-|t|} \frac{\sin(x - t)}{x - t} dt.

Therefore, the functions are f(x) = (1/2)e^{-|x|} and g(x) = sin x/(π x), and h(x) is their convolution.

I think that's the answer. Let me just check if there's another possible factorization. Suppose someone might think of different functions, but given the forms, I think this is the standard approach. The key is recognizing that the given \(\widehat{h}(\omega)\) is the product of a Lorentzian (1/(1 + ω²)) and a rectangular function (which is 1 between -1 and 1). So each of these corresponds to a known Fourier transform, hence their product is the Fourier transform of the convolution. So yes, I believe that's the correct solution.
</think>To express the given Fourier transform \(\widehat{h}(\omega)\) as a product of two familiar Fourier transforms \(\widehat{f}(\omega)\) and \(\widehat{g}(\omega)\), we start by identifying the functions whose Fourier transforms match the given form.

The given Fourier transform is:
\[
\widehat{h}(\omega) = \begin{cases} 
\frac{1}{1+\omega^2} & \text{if } |\omega| < 1 \\
0 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
\]

This can be written as the product of two functions:
\[
\widehat{h}(\omega) = \frac{1}{1+\omega^2} \cdot \text{rect}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)
\]
where \(\text{rect}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)\) is the rectangular function that is 1 for \(|\omega| < 1\) and 0 otherwise.

Next, we identify the functions \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\) whose Fourier transforms are \(\frac{1}{1+\omega^2}\) and \(\text{rect}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)\), respectively.

1. **Identifying \(f(x)\):**
   The Fourier transform of \(f(x) = \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|}\) is:
   \[
   \widehat{f}(\omega) = \frac{1}{1+\omega^2}
   \]
   This can be verified by computing the Fourier transform of \(f(x)\):
   \[
   \widehat{f}(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|} e^{-i\omega x} \, dx
   \]
   Splitting the integral into two parts:
   \[
   \widehat{f}(\omega) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \int_{-\infty}^{0} e^{x} e^{-i\omega x} \, dx + \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-x} e^{-i\omega x} \, dx \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left( \int_{-\infty}^{0} e^{x(1-i\omega)} \, dx + \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-x(1+i\omega)} \, dx \right)
   \]
   Evaluating these integrals:
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left( \left[ \frac{e^{x(1-i\omega)}}{1-i\omega} \right]_{-\infty}^{0} + \left[ \frac{e^{-x(1+i\omega)}}{-1-i\omega} \right]_{0}^{\infty} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{1}{1-i\omega} + \frac{1}{1+i\omega} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{(1+i\omega) + (1-i\omega)}{(1-i\omega)(1+i\omega)} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{2}{1+\omega^2} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{1+\omega^2}
   \]

2. **Identifying \(g(x)\):**
   The Fourier transform of \(g(x) = \frac{\sin x}{\pi x}\) is:
   \[
   \widehat{g}(\omega) = \text{rect}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}\right)
   \]
   This can be verified by computing the inverse Fourier transform of the rectangular function:
   \[
   g(x) = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-1}^{1} e^{i\omega x} \, d\omega
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2\pi} \left[ \frac{e^{i\omega x}}{ix} \right]_{-1}^{1}
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2\pi} \left( \frac{e^{ix} - e^{-ix}}{ix} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2\pi} \left( \frac{2i \sin x}{ix} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{\sin x}{\pi x}
   \]

Therefore, the functions \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\) are:
\[
f(x) = \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|}

    10	Yet another library to generate OpenAPI document and validate request & response with Python annotations.

    12	## Features
    13	
    14	* Less boilerplate code, only annotations, no need for YAML :sparkles:
    15	* Generate API document with [Redoc UI](https://github.com/Redocly/redoc) or [Swagger UI](https://github.com/swagger-api/swagger-ui) :yum:

<#sep#>You are given a string `s` consisting of words separated by spaces. Implement a function `sort_by_length` that sorts the words in the string by their length in ascending order. If two words have the same length, they should be sorted alphabetically. Return the sorted list of words.

**Input:**
- A single string `s` (1 ≤ |s| ≤ 1000), where |s| is the length of the string. The string contains only lowercase English letters and spaces.

**Output:**
- A list of words sorted by length and alphabetically if lengths are equal.

**Sample Input:**
```
"the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
['the', 'dog', 'fox', 'the', 'lazy', 'over', 'brown', 'jumps']
```

```python
def sort_by_length(s):
    """
    Sorts the words in the string by their length in ascending order.
    If two words have the same length, they are sorted alphabetically.
    
    :param s: A string of words separated by spaces.
    :return: A list of words sorted by length and alphabetically if lengths are equal.
    """
    words = s.split()
    sorted_words = sorted(words, key=lambda word: (len(word), word))
    return sorted_words

# Example usage
input_string = "the quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog"
sorted_list = sort_by_length(input_string)
print(sorted_list)
```<#sep#> \left( \frac{e^{ix} - e^{-ix}}{ix} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{1}{2\pi} \left( \frac{2i \sin x}{ix} \right)
   \]
   \[
   = \frac{\sin x}{\pi x}
   \]

Therefore, the functions \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\) are:
\[
f(x) = \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|}
\]
\[
g(x) = \frac{\sin x}{\pi x}
\]

Finally, \(h(x)\) is the convolution of \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\):
\[
h(x) = (f * g)(x) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f(t) g(x - t) \, dt
\]
\[
= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{1}{2} e^{-|t|} \frac{\sin(x - t)}{\pi (x - t)} \, dt
\]
\[
= \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-|t|} \frac{\sin(x - t)}{x - t} \, dt
\]

Thus, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{h(x) = \left( \frac{1}{2} e^{-|x|} \right) * \left( \frac{\sin x}{\pi x} \right)}
